The connecting homomorphism for Hermitian K K -theory: Projective bundles and Grassmannians Thanks: MSC classes: 19G38, 11E81, and 14M15.
Tao Huang, Heng Xie

Abstract
. We provide a geometric interpretation for the connecting homomorphism in the localization sequence of Hermitian K K -theory. As an application, we compute the Hermitian K K -theory of projective bundles and Grassmannians in the regular case. We provide an explicit basis for Hermitian K K -theory of Grassmannians, which is indexed by even Young diagrams together with another special class of Young diagrams, that we call buffalo-check Young diagrams. To achieve this, we develop pushforwards and pullbacks in Hermitian K K -theory using Grothendieck’s residue complexes, and we establish fundamental theorems for those pushforwards and pullbacks, including base change, projection, and excess intersection formulas.



1.
Introduction

Pushforwards and pullbacks are foundational in many cohomology theories. For instance, in
K
-theory, Chow theory, and Witt theory, they serve as powerful computational tools. Interestingly, pushforwards in Witt theory always keep track of orientations, complicating computations compared to those in oriented cohomology theories, cf.
[
CH11
]
. Based on pushforwards and pullbacks, Balmer and Calmès
[
BC09
]
introduced the ‘Blow-up setup’, a method that geometrically interprets the connecting homomorphism in the 12-term localization sequence for Witt groups. As an application, Balmer-Calmès computed Witt groups of Grassmannians, which was a long standing open problem in the Witt theory, cf.
[
BC12
]
.

Hermitian
K
-theory, a generalization of Witt theory, has garnered attention for its applications in recent works. Roughly speaking, Hermitian
K
-theory can be thought of a ‘combination’ of
K
-theory and Witt theory in view of the algebraic Bott sequence. We mention some recent applications of Hermitian
K
-theory. Asok and Fasel utilized Hermitian
K
-theory as a tool to give a cohomological classification of vector bundles of rank
2
on a smooth affine threefold
[
AF14
]
, and they also made progress in Murthy’s conjecture
[
AF15
]
. Fasel and Srinivas
[
FS09
]
showed that a vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
of rank
3
over a smooth affine threefold splits off a trivial direct summand if and only if its Euler class
e(\mathcal{V})
in Hermitian
K
-theory is zero. By studying the slice filtration for Hermitian
K
-theory, Röndigs, Spitzweck, and Østvær computed the first stable homotopy groups of motivic spheres
[
RSØ19
]
. The second author showed that a numerical condition of Atiyah on Hurwitz’s 1898 problem can be generalized from
\mathbb{R}
to any field by computing Hermitian
K
-theory of deleted quadrics
[
Xie14
]
.

This work is devoted to the study of pushforwards and pullbacks in Hermitian
K
-theory and the geometric description of the connecting homomorphism in Hermitian
K
-theory. Building on the work of Schlichting
[
Sch10
]
and
[
Sch17
]
in Hermitian
K
-theory, we show that the connecting homomorphism in the localization sequence of Hermitian
K
-theory can be interpreted by the pushforward, pullback and cupping with the Bott element. More precisely, we have the following result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that we have the following diagram:

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{v}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\iota}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{Y}

where
\iota:Z\to X
is a regular immersion of codimension
c\geq 2
between regular schemes,
B
is the blow up of
X
along
Z
, and
E
is the exceptional fibre. Here, we set
U:=X-Z\cong B-E
, and let
v,\tilde{v}
be the corresponding open immersions. Suppose that there is a flat morphism
\tilde{\alpha}:B\to Y
such that
\alpha:=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{v}
is an affine bundle.
Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. Then, there exists an integer
\lambda(L)\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(A)

If
\lambda(L)\equiv c-1\mod 2
, then we have an equivalence

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,L)\simeq\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y,L_{Y})\oplus\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c]}(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes L_{Z})

of spectra in the stable homotopy category of spectra
\mathcal{SH}
.

(B)

If
\lambda(L)\equiv c\mod 2
, then the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(U,L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha^{*})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c]}(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes L)[1]}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c+1]}(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes L)}
\scriptstyle{\eta\cup}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y,L_{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\iota}^{*}\tilde{\alpha}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(E,L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(E,\omega_{\widetilde{\pi}}\otimes\widetilde{\pi}^{*}(\omega_{\iota}\otimes L))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi}_{*}}

of spectra is commutative in
\mathcal{SH}
. In particular, on the level of homotopy groups, the connecting homomorphism

\partial:\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}(U,L)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c]}_{i}(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes L)[1]

is equal to the composition
(\eta\cup)\widetilde{\pi}_{*}\tilde{\iota}^{*}\tilde{\alpha}(\alpha^{*})^{-1}
.

Pushforwards and pullbacks in Hermitian
K
-theory which were conjectured to exist should be of independent interests, cf.
[
BW23
, Section 3.3]
.
To demonstrate the applications of Theorem
1.1
, we compute Hermitian
K
-theory of projective bundles in Theorem
9.2
. This computation has previously appeared in the work of Walter in the case of
\mathrm{GW}_{0}
, but using a different method (see also
[
Roh21
]
,
[
KSW21
]
and
[
CHN24
]
). Furthermore, we proceed to compute Hermitian
K
-theory of Grassmannians in Section
10
.

Let
S
be a regular scheme, and let
L
be a line bundle over
S
. Let
\mathrm{Gr}_{d}(m)
be the Grassmannian of
d
-dimensional subbundles of the trivial bundle
\mathcal{O}^{d+m}
, and let
\Delta_{d}
be the determinantal line bundle of the taotaulogical
d
-bundle on
\mathrm{Gr}_{d}(m)
. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}(\mathrm{Gr}_{d}(m),L)^{\mathrm{tot}}:=\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}(\mathrm{Gr}_{d}(m),L)\oplus\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}(\mathrm{Gr}_{d}(m),L\otimes\Delta_{d}).

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
10.45
)
.

The map

(\Theta^{0}+\Theta^{1},\Omega^{0}+\Omega^{1}):\bigoplus_{(\Lambda,c_{\Lambda})\in\mathfrak{B}_{d,m}}K_{i}(S)\oplus\bigoplus_{\Pi\in\mathfrak{A}_{d,m}}\mathrm{GW}^{[n-|\Pi|]}_{i}(S,L)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}(\mathrm{Gr}_{d}(m),L)^{\mathrm{tot}}

is an equivalence of spectra, where
\mathfrak{A}_{d,m}
is the set of even Young diagrams in
(d\times m)
-frame, and
\mathfrak{B}_{d,m}
is the set of buffalo-check Young diagrams in
(d\times m)
-frame. Here,
|\Pi|
denotes the number of boxes in an even Young diagram
\Pi
.

Even Young diagrams were introduced by Balmer-Calmès in order to index the additive basis for Witt groups of Grassmannian (cf.
[
BC12
, Definition 2.7]
). To understand the additive basis for the
K
-theory part of Hermitian
K
-theory of Grassmannians, we shall introduce a combinatorial object called
buffalo-check
Young diagrams.

Consider an ordinary Young diagram sitting in the upper left corner of a
(d\times m)
-frame
\Xi
, which is a rectangle consisting of
d
rows and
m
columns. The
boundary

b(\Lambda)
of a Young diagram
\Lambda
in
\Xi
is a lattice path that goes from lower-left to upper-right corner in
\Xi
such that the Young diagram
\Lambda
lives precisely in the upper-left area above the path. The boundary
b(\Lambda)
consists of
segments

s_{1},\ldots,s_{l}
, where we order them from left to right.

\Lambda
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{4}
s_{5}
\ldots

Figure 1.
Young diagram
\Lambda
in the
(d\times m)
-frame
\Xi
. The boundary
b(\Lambda)
is thickened.

A Young diagram
\Lambda
in a
(d\times m)
-frame
\Xi
is called
K
-even
if there exists an integer
1\leq w(\Lambda)<l
such that segments
s_{2},\ldots,s_{w(\Lambda)-1}
have even length and the segment
s_{w(\Lambda)}
, which has odd length if
w(\Lambda)\geq 2
, is vertical. In other words, a Young diagram
\Lambda
in
(d\times m)
-frame
\Xi
is
K
-even if and only if (i) the first segment
s_{1}
is vertical, or if (ii) there exists an integer
r(\Lambda)\geq 2
such that the segments
s_{2},\ldots,s_{r(\Lambda)-1}
have even length and the segement
s_{r(\Lambda)}
is vertical of odd length. A box in
\Xi
is called a
center
of a
K
-even Young diagram
\Lambda
, if it sits in the angle between the segments
s_{w(\Lambda)}
and
s_{w(\Lambda)+1}
; Or equivalently, if it sits in the angle between the segments
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
when condition (i) holds, or between the segments
s_{r(\Lambda)}
and
s_{r(\Lambda)+1}
when condition (ii) holds. Note that a
K
-even Young diagram
\Lambda
in
\Xi
could have at most two centers.

Figure 2.
Five examples of
K
-even Young diagrams with centers hatched and boundaries thickened.

We order the rows (resp. columns) in
\Xi
from left to right (resp. bottom to top). Observe that the centers of
K
-even Young diagrams can not locate on thoses boxes in the intersection of even rows and even columns, and therefore the boxes that
K
-even Young diagrams can center form a buffulo-check pattern, see Figure
3
. A
buffalo-check Young diagram
in
\Xi
is a pair
(\Lambda,c_{\Lambda})
consisting of a
K
-even Young diagram
\Lambda
in
\Xi
and a center
c_{\Lambda}
of
\Lambda
.

Figure 3.
An example of the buffalo-check pattern of size
(6\times 6)
. The centers of
K
-even Young diagrams can only sit on those non-white boxes. A black box (resp. grey box) is the location of the center of a buffalo-check Young diagram, if the corresponding basis element lands in the even (resp. odd) twist.

In Section
10
, we will describe the underlying map of the isomorphism in Theorem
10.45
in light of the combinatorial of even and buffalo-check Young diagrams.
If
S=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C})
, our result agrees with
[
Zib11
]
. To compare, we note that

|\mathfrak{B}_{d,m}|=\binom{d+m}{d}-\binom{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor}{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}.

Taking the negative homotopy groups, we recover Balmer-Calmès’s computation on Witt groups of Grassmannians
[
BC12
]
. Hermitian
K
-theory of Grassmannians could also be computed using the method of semi-orthogonal decomposition, as done by Walter in the case of projective bundles. This approach has been pursued in recent work
[
Roh21
]
, where the author addresses half of the cases and assumes that the base is of characteristic zero. It is important to note that these restrictions are not necessary within our method.

The recent advancements in Hermitian
K
-theory, especially when the invertibility of the element two in the base is not assumed, have opened new avenues for exploration, cf.
[
Sch21
]
and
[
CDH+23
]
. If we assume that two is invertible in the base, then one has the freedom to choose either the framework of Hermitian
K
-theory of dg categories
[
Sch17
]
or that of the infinity category
[
CDH+23
]
to work on. It is interesting to investigate our results in this paper when two is not invertible in the base.

Convention
. Throughout this paper, we assume that every scheme is separated Noetherian of finite Krull dimension, over a commutative ring
k
and has
\frac{1}{2}
in its global sections.

2.
Hermitian
K
-theory of schemes

Let
X
be a scheme.
Let
\mathbf{M}(X)
be the category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, and let
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
be the dg category of bounded complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. The pair

\big(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\mathrm{quis}\big)

is a pretriangulated dg category with weak equivalences, where
\mathrm{quis}\subset Z^{0}\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
is the set of quasi-isomorphisms of complexes, cf.
[
Sch17
, Section 9.5]
. It’s associated triangulated category

\mathcal{T}\big(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\mathrm{quis}\big)

is the derived category
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
. Let
I^{\bullet}
be an object of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
. Define the duality functor on
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
as

\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}:\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))^{\mathrm{op}}\to\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X)):\quad A^{\bullet}\mapsto\left[A^{\bullet},I^{\bullet}\right]_{X}

where
[A^{\bullet},I^{\bullet}]_{X}
is the mapping complex of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules defined in
[
Sch10
, Section 7.2]
. Define the natural transformation

\textnormal{can}:1\rightarrow\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}

by the formula

\textnormal{can}_{A}:A^{\bullet}\rightarrow(A^{\bullet})^{\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}}:m\mapsto\big(\textnormal{can}_{A}(m):f\mapsto(-1)^{|f||m|}f(m)\big).

We usually omit the bullet in the notation of complexes, and write
A=A^{\bullet}
if no confusion occurs.

2.1.
Coherent Hermitian
K
-theory

Let
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
be the full dg subcategory of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
consisting of bounded complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules with coherent cohomology.

Definition 2.2

(
[
Har66
, p.304]
)
.

A
residue complex
on
X
is a bounded complex
I^{\bullet}
of quasi-coherent injective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, with coherent cohomology, and such that there is an isomorphism

I^{j}\cong\bigoplus_{\mu_{I}(x)=j}i_{x}\left(E(\kappa(x))\right)

for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\mu_{I}:X\to\mathbb{Z}
is the codimension function (cf.
[
Har66
, Section V.7, p.282]
),
E(\kappa(x))
is the injective hull of residue field
\kappa(x)
over the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
and
i_{x}(E(\kappa(x)))
is the skyscraper sheaf at
x
with value
E(\kappa(x))
. Denote the full additive subcategory of
Z^{0}\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
of residue complexes by
\mathrm{Res}(X)
.

Definition 2.3

(
[
Har66
, p.258]
)
.

A
dualizing complex

K^{\bullet}
on
X
is a complex of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules in
D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
which is isomorphic to a complex of injective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules

I^{\bullet}:=(\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow I^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}I^{m+1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow I^{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{n-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}I^{n}\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots)\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))

such that the morphism of complexes
\textnormal{can}_{A}:A^{\bullet}\rightarrow(A^{\bullet})^{\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}\sharp_{I^{\bullet}}}
is an isomorphism for any
A^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
.

Remark 2.4
.

(i)

Since we assume that every scheme is noetherian of finite Krull dimension, a residue complex is dualizing cf.
[
Har66
, Proposition VI.1.1 (c), p.304]
(Note that a pointwise dualizing complex is dualizing
[
Har66
, Proposition V.8.2, p.288]
in this case).

(ii)

The terminology “dualizing complex” used by
[
Sch17
]
,
[
Gil07
]
and
[
Xie20
]
is precisely a degreewise injective dualizing complex. A dualizing complex
I^{\bullet}
is called
minimal
if it is degreewise injective and
I^{r}
is an essential extension of
\ker(d^{r})
for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that a degreewise injective dualizing complex is a residue complex if and only if it is minimal, cf.
[
Gil07
, Remark 1.16]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Suppose that
I
is a residue complex on
X
. Then, the quadruple

\left(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{I},\textnormal{can}\right)

is a dg category with weak equivalences and duality.

Proof.

See
[
Sch17
, Section 9.5]
.
∎

This result leads to the following definition.

Definition 2.6

(
[
Sch17
, Definition 9.15]
)
.

Let
I
be a residue complex on
X
. The
n
-th shifted coherent Grothendieck-Witt spectrum of
X
with coefficients in
I
is the
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt spectrum

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,I\right):=\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\big(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{I},\textnormal{can}\big).

Its homotopy groups are denoted by

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}\left(X,I\right):=\pi_{i}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,I\right)

for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\iota:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion and
I
a residue complex on
X
.
Define
\textnormal{Ch}_{c,Z}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
to be the full dg subcategory of
\textnormal{Ch}_{c}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
consisting of objects with cohomology supported in
Z
. The
n
-th shifted coherent Grothendieck-Witt spectrum of
X
supported on
Z
with coefficients in
I
is the
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt spectrum

\mathrm{GW}_{Z}^{[n]}\left(X,I\right):=\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\big(\textnormal{Ch}_{c,Z}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{I},\textnormal{can}\big).

Theorem 2.8

(Localization)
.

Suppose that
X
is a scheme admitting a dualizing complex
I
. Let
\iota:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion, and let
v:U=X-Z\hookrightarrow X
be its open complement. Then for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sequence

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{Z}(X,I)\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,I)\xrightarrow{v^{\ast}}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(U,v^{\ast}I)\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{Z}(X,I)[1]

is a distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{SH}
.

Proof.

See
[
Sch17
, Theorem 9.19]
.
∎

2.9.
Hermitian
K
-theory of vector bundles

Let
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{V}(X))
be the full dg subcategory of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
consisting of complexes of vector bundles over
X
of finite rank.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
L
be an line bundle. Then, the quadruple

\left(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}\left(\mathbf{V}(X)\right),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{L[m]},\textnormal{can}\right)

is a dg category with weak equivalences and duality, where
L[m]
is the complex consisting of
L
concentrated in degree
-m
.

Proof.

See
[
Sch17
, Section 9.1]
.
∎

Definition 2.11

(
[
Sch17
, Definition 9.1]
)
.

Let
X
be a scheme with an ample family of line bundles and let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. The
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt spectrum of
X
with coefficients in
L[m]
is the
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt spectrum

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,L[m]\right):=\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\big(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}\left(\mathbf{V}(X)\right),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{L[m]},\textnormal{can}\big).

Its homotopy groups are denoted by

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}\left(X,L[m]\right):=\pi_{i}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,L[m]\right)

for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. When
n=0
, or
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, we may omit the notation corresponding to it. For instance,
\mathrm{GW}(X)
(resp.
\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X)
) represents
\mathrm{GW}^{[0]}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
(resp.
\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
).

Remark 2.12
.

Note that there is a natural stable equivalence
\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,L)\simeq\mathrm{GW}^{[0]}(X,L[n])
.

Suppose now that
X
is a regular scheme, and
L
is an line bundle. Then there is a residue complex
I
on
X
resolving
L
, and we denote
\rho:L\to I
the canonical quasi-isomorphism of complexes.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
X
be a regular scheme. Then, the inclusion form functor

(\mathrm{id},\rho):\left(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}\left(\mathbf{V}(X)\right),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{L},\textnormal{can}\right)\to\left(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{I},\textnormal{can}\right)

with duality compatibility morphism
\rho:\sharp_{L}\to\sharp_{I}
induces a stable equivalence

\rho:\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,L\right)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,I\right).

of Grothendieck-Witt spectra.

Proof.

See
[
Sch17
, Theorem 9.18]
.
∎

Let
X
be a scheme with an ample family of line bundles and let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. Let
Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion. Let
\textnormal{Ch}_{Z}^{b}(\mathbf{V}(X))
be the dg full subcategory of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{V}(X))
consisting of objects which has cohomology supported on
Z
.

Definition 2.14
.

The
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt spectrum of
X
supported on
Z
with coefficients in
L[m]
is the
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt spectrum

\mathrm{GW}_{Z}^{[n]}\left(X,L[m]\right):=\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\big(\textnormal{Ch}_{Z}^{b}\left(\mathbf{V}(X)\right),\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{L[m]},\big).

Theorem 2.15

(Dévissage)
.

Let
X
be a regular scheme and
\iota:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a regular immersion of codimension
c
. Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. Then the pushforward is an equivalence of spectra

\iota_{\ast}:\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c]}\left(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes\iota^{\ast}L\right)\to\mathrm{GW}_{Z}^{[n]}\left(X,L\right),

where
\omega_{\iota}
is the relative canonical line bundle.

Proof.

See
[
Xie20
, Theorem 6.1]
.
∎

Theorem 2.16

(Localization)
.

Let
\iota:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a regular immersion of regular schemes of codimension
c
, and let
v:U=X\backslash Z\to X
be its open complement. For any line bundle
L
on
X
, the sequence

\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c]}(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes\iota^{\ast}L)\xrightarrow{\iota_{\ast}}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,L)\xrightarrow{v^{\ast}}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(U,L_{U})\xrightarrow{\partial}\mathrm{GW}^{[n-c]}(Z,\omega_{\iota}\otimes\iota^{\ast}L)[1]

is a distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{SH}
.

Proof.

In view of Theorem
2.15
, the results follows from
[
Sch17
, Theorem 6.6]
.
∎

Theorem 2.17

(Algebraic Bott sequence)
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
. The sequence of spectra

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,L)\xrightarrow{F}K(X)\xrightarrow{H}\mathrm{GW}^{[n+1]}(X,L)\xrightarrow{\eta\cup}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,L)[1]

is a distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{SH}
.

Proof.

See
[
Sch17
, Theorem 6.1]
.
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
L,L^{\prime}
be line bundles on
X
. The diagram

\textstyle{K_{i}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\scriptstyle{\cup F(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}_{i}(X,L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup\alpha}
\textstyle{K_{i+j}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n+m]}_{i+j}(X,L\otimes L^{\prime})}

commutes for any
\alpha\in\mathrm{GW}^{[m]}_{j}(X,L^{\prime})
. In particular, the composition

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,L^{\prime})\xrightarrow{F}K(X)\xrightarrow{H}\mathrm{GW}^{[n+1]}(X,L\otimes L^{\prime})

is homotopic to
\beta\cup-
, where
\beta=H(1)\in\mathrm{GW}^{[1]}_{0}(X,L)
is the image of
1\in K_{0}(X)
under the the map
H:K(X)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[1]}(X,L)
.

Proof.

The result follows from the multiplicativity of the algebraic Bott sequence, cf.
[
Sch17
, Proof of Theorem 6.1]
.
∎

3.
Pushforwards for Hermitian
K
-theory

3.1.
Residue complexes

In this subsection, we review the theory of residue complexes, cf.
[
Har66
]
and
[
Con00
]
.

Theorem 3.2

(Residue theorem
[
Har66
]
)
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper morphism. Then, there is a functor of additive categories

f^{\Delta}:\mathrm{Res}(Y)\to\mathrm{Res}(X)

together with a natural transformation
\mathrm{Tr}:f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}\to 1
(so called trace map) from
\mathrm{Res}(Y)
to
Z^{0}\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))
such that the following conditions hold

(T1)

For every residue complex
J\in\mathrm{Res}(Y)
, denote by
\zeta:f_{\ast}\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J}\to\sharp_{J}f_{\ast}
the composition

f_{\ast}\left[-,f^{\Delta}J\right]_{X}\xrightarrow{\phi}\left[f_{\ast}(-),f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}J\right]_{Y}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[f_{\ast}(-),J\right]_{Y}

where
\phi
is the canonical morphism, cf.
[
Lip09
, (3.1.4), p.86 ]
. Then,

\zeta_{A}:f_{\ast}\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J}(A)\to\sharp_{J}f_{\ast}(A)

is a quasi-isomorphism for each
f_{*}
-acyclic complex
A
in
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
.

(T2)

If
f
and
g
are two consecutive proper morphisms, then there is a natural isomorphism

c:f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}\to(gf)^{\Delta}

satisfying associativity, together with a commutative diagram

\textstyle{g_{\ast}f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{(gf)_{\ast}(gf)^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{g_{\ast}g^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}.}

Proof.

For the existence of
f^{\Delta}
, see
[
Har66
, Theorem VI.3.1, p.318]
. For the existence of
\mathrm{Tr}
, see
[
Har66
, Theorem VI.4.2, p.339 and VII.2.1, p.369]
or
[
Con00
, Theorem 3.4.1, p.147]
. For the proof of (T1), see
[
Har66
, Theorem VII.3.3, p.379]
. For the proof of (T2), See
[
Har66
, Theorem VI.4.2(TRA1), p.340]
. Note the subtle point is that
(gf)_{\ast}=g_{\ast}f_{\ast}
.
∎

3.3.
Pushforward

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper morphism throughout this section unless otherwise specified. The aim of this subsection is to prove the following result.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
J
be a residue complex on
Y
. Then, the dg functor

f_{\ast}:\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))\to\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))

induces a map of Grothendieck-Witt spectra

f_{\ast}:\mathrm{\mathrm{GW}}^{[n]}\left(X,f^{\Delta}J\right)\to\mathrm{\mathrm{GW}}^{[n]}\left(Y,J\right)

which respects composition, i.e. if
f
and
g
are two consecutive morphisms, then
g_{\ast}f_{\ast}=(gf)_{\ast}
.

Remark 3.5
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper map. Then
\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}
maps complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules with coherent cohomology to complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules with coherent cohomology, as shown in
[
EGA3
, Chapter III Theorem 3.2.1]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of schemes, and let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Then, the following diagram

\textstyle{f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}\textnormal{can}}
can
\textstyle{f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(f_{\ast}\mathcal{F},f_{\ast}\mathcal{G}),f_{\ast}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),f_{\ast}\mathcal{G})}

commutes in
\mathbf{M}(X)
.

Proof.

We check the commutativity locally by sections. Let
V
be an open subset of
Y
. We need to check the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(f^{-1}V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
can
can
\textstyle{\textnormal{Hom}_{f^{-1}V}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})|_{f^{-1}V},\mathcal{G}|_{f^{-1}V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\textnormal{Hom}_{V}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(f_{\ast}\mathcal{F},f_{\ast}\mathcal{G})|_{V},f_{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\textnormal{Hom}_{V}(f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})|_{V},f_{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{V})}

commutes.

Let
s
be a section in
\mathcal{F}(f^{-1}V)
. We check that
\phi(\textnormal{can}(s))=\phi^{\sharp}(\textnormal{can}(s))
. On the left-hand side, the morphism of
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-modules
\phi(\textnormal{can}(s)):f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})|_{V}\to f_{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{V}
may be described as follows. Take an open subset
U\subseteq V
,
\phi(\textnormal{can})(s)(U)
sends an element
t\in\textnormal{Hom}_{f^{-1}(U)}(\mathcal{F}|_{f^{-1}U},\mathcal{G}|_{f^{-1}U})
to
t(f^{-1}U)(s|_{f^{-1}U})
in
\mathcal{G}(f^{-1}U)
. For the right-hand side, the morphism
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-modules
\phi^{\sharp}(\textnormal{can}(s)):f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})|_{V}\to f_{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{V}
sends
t
to
\phi(t)(f^{-1}U)(s|_{f^{-1}U})
. The morphism
\phi(t)(f^{-1}U)
is equal to
t(f^{-1}U)
in
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}(f^{-1}U)}(\mathcal{F}(f^{-1}(U)),\mathcal{G}(f^{-1}(U)))
, cf.
[
Lip09
, p. 86 (3.1.4)]
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, and let
\psi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be a morphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Then, the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
can
can
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}),\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}

commutes.

Proof.

Exercise.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

The pushforward functor

f_{\ast}:\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))\to\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))

induces a dg form functor

(f_{\ast},\zeta):(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J},\textnormal{can})\to(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\sharp_{J},\textnormal{can}).

Proof.

It is enough to check that the following diagram

\textstyle{f_{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}\left(\mathrm{can}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}\left[\left[A,f^{\Delta}J\right],f^{\Delta}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{A^{\sharp}}}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f_{\ast}A,J\right],J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{A}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\left[f_{\ast}\left[A,f^{\Delta}J\right],J\right]}

commutes for every
A\in\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
. For the commutativity of this diagram, we depict the all the maps by definition in the following diagram.

\textstyle{f_{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}\left(\mathrm{can}\right)}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}\left[\left[A,f^{\Delta}J\right],f^{\Delta}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f_{\ast}A,f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}J\right],f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}
\textstyle{\left[f_{\ast}\left[A,f^{\Delta}J\right],f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f_{\ast}A,J\right],J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{Tr}}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f_{\ast}A,f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}J\right],J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\left[f_{\ast}\left[A,f^{\Delta}J\right],J\right].}

The diagram
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:push_dual_1}$}}
(resp.
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:push_dual_2}$}}
) is commutative by Lemma
3.6
(resp. Lemma
3.7
). The diagram
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:push_dual_3}$}}
commutes by naturality.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

If
f
is finite, the pushforward functor is exact and
\zeta
is a quasi-isomorphism. Consequently, the dg form functor
(f_{\ast},\zeta)
is non-singular and exact in the sense of
[
Sch10
]
. Thus, the dg form functor
(f_{\ast},\zeta)
induces a map of Grothendieck-Witt spectra
\mathrm{GW}^{n}(X,f^{\Delta}J)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y,J)
. However, if
f
is not finite, the above statement may not hold true. This limitation is evident in the special case of Witt groups. To construct the pushforward for Witt groups, one needs to use the right-derived functor
\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}
, as noted in
[
CH11
, Theorem 4.4]
. In the context of higher algebraic
G
-theory, one might employ a technique from Thomason-Trobaugh
[
TT90
, 3.16]
to construct a pushforward of
K
-theory as the composition:

G(X)\xleftarrow{\cong}K(\mathcal{F}_{X})\xrightarrow{f_{\ast}}G(Y),

where
\mathcal{F}_{X}\subset\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
denotes the category of complexes of flasque
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Regrettably, the category
\mathcal{F}_{X}
may not be closed under the duality
\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J}
. The issue persists even after replacing
\mathcal{F}_{X}
with the subcategory of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
consisting of degree-wise injective (or
f_{\ast}
-acyclic) complexes, as considered in
[
Tho86
, 1.13]
. To overcome this problem, we introduce the subsequent construction.

Definition 3.10
.

The full dg subcategory
\mathcal{A}_{f}
of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
is defined to be

\mathcal{A}_{f}:=\left\{A\in\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))\,\middle|\,\theta_{A}:f_{\ast}(A)\to\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A)\text{ is an isomorphism. }\right\}

where
\theta:f_{\ast}\to\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}
is the canonical natural transformation from
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
to
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The full dg subcategory
\mathcal{A}_{f}
is pretriangulated.

Proof.

We need to check that
H^{0}\mathcal{A}_{f}
is triangulated with respect to the split exact triangulated structure. It’s clear that
0\in H^{0}\mathcal{A}_{f}
and if
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, so is
A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
. Therefore
H^{0}\mathcal{A}_{f}
is additive. It’s clear that if
A\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, so is
A[1]
.
Next, for any
A_{1}\simeq A_{2}\in\mathrm{K}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
such that
A_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
, the diagram

\textstyle{f_{\ast}(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}(A_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{2})}

commutes and thus
A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
. For any distinguished triangle
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},f,g,h)
in
\mathrm{K}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
such that
A_{1},A_{2}\in H^{0}\mathcal{A}_{f}
, the canonical natural transformation
\theta:f_{\ast}\to\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}
induces the following map of distinguished triangles

\textstyle{f_{\ast}(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}(A_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}(A_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}(A_{1})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(f)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(g)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(h)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{1})[1].}

Consequently, since all the other vertical arrows in the diagram are isomorphisms, the map
\theta:f_{\ast}(A_{3})\to\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(A_{3})
is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
I
be a residue complex on
X
, then
\sharp_{I}(\mathcal{A}_{f})\subset\mathcal{A}_{f}
, i.e. the duality functor
\sharp_{I}
preserves the dg category
\mathcal{A}_{f}
.

Proof.

Since
I
is injective and bounded,
I
is
K
-injective. For every object
A
of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
, the mapping complex
[A,I]_{X}
is weakly
K
-injective
[
Spa88
, Proposition 5.14]
, thus the map
\theta:f_{\ast}(\sharp_{I}(A))\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{R}f_{\ast}(\sharp_{I}(A))
is an isomorphism, cf.
[
Spa88
, Proposition 6.7]
, i.e.
\sharp_{I}(A)\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Note that the above proof implies that the full dg subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{X}
of weakly
K
-injective complexes in
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
is also closed under the duality
\sharp_{I}
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

By Lemma
3.11
and
3.12
, the quadruple
(\mathcal{A}_{f},\mathrm{quis},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J},\mathrm{can})
forms a pretriangulated dg category with weak equivalences and duality. Furthermore, the composition of dg form functors

(\mathcal{A}_{f},\textnormal{quis},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J})\hookrightarrow(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\textnormal{quis},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J})\xrightarrow{(f_{\ast},\zeta)}(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\textnormal{quis},\sharp_{J})

is non-singular and exact, and therefore induces a map

f_{\ast}:\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(\mathcal{A}_{f},\textnormal{quis},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J}\right)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,J\right)

of Grothendieck-Witt spectra
[
Sch10
, Section 2.8]
.

We claim that the inclusion of dg categories with weak equivalences
\mathcal{A}_{f}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
induces an equivalence
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{f},\mathrm{quis})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
of associated triangulated categories. To see the essential surjectivity, for any object
A
in
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
, we have a canonical quasi-isomorphism
\textnormal{can}:A\to\sharp_{I}\sharp_{I}(A)
for any residue complex
I
on
X
, and we could take
I=f^{\Delta}J
.
It follows that the inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{f}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
induces a stable equivalence

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(\mathcal{A}_{f},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J}\right)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,f^{\Delta}J\right)

of Grothendieck-Witt spectra by
[
Sch17
, Theorem 6.5]
.

The required pushforward morphism

f_{\ast}:\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,f^{\Delta}J\right)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,J\right)

is defined to be the composition

\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,f^{\Delta}J\right)\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(\mathcal{A}_{f},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J}\right)\xrightarrow{f_{\ast}}\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,J\right).

The fact that the pushforward morphism respects composition follows from Proposition
3.14
below.
∎

Proposition 3.14

(Composition of pushforward)
.

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be proper morphisms. Let
K
be a residue complex on
Z
. Then, the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,(gf)^{\Delta}K\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(gf)_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,g^{\Delta}K\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Z,K\right)}

commutes.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{X}
be the full dg subcategory of
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
consisting of objects that are weakly
K
-injective. Note that
\mathcal{W}_{X}
is pretriangulated, cf.
[
Spa88
, Proposition 5.15 (a)]
. By Remark
3.13
, the category
\mathcal{W}_{X}
is closed under the duality
\sharp_{I}
for any residue complex
I
on
X
. Moreover, the inclusion
\mathcal{W}_{X}\subset\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
induces an equivalence
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{W}_{X},\mathrm{quis})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
of the associated triangulated category.
By
[
Spa88
, Proposition 5.15 (b)]
, the functor
f_{\ast}
maps
\mathcal{W}_{X}
into
\mathcal{W}_{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{g}
.

To show the commutativity claimed in the proposition, we consider the following diagram

\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\sharp_{f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id},c)}
\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\sharp_{(gf)^{\Delta}K})}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{A}_{f},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f_{\ast},\zeta)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\mathcal{W}_{X},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 4$}}{}}
\scriptstyle{(f_{\ast},\zeta)}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id},c)}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{W}_{X},\sharp_{(gf)^{\Delta}K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{((gf)_{\ast},\zeta)}
\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\sharp_{g^{\Delta}K})}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{A}_{g},\sharp_{g^{\Delta}K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g_{\ast},\zeta)}
\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Z)),\sharp_{K}).}

It is enough to prove that the square
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:push_comp}$}}
commutes, that is to show the following diagram

\textstyle{(gf)_{\ast}\left[A,f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{(gf)_{\ast}\left[A,(gf)^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{g_{\ast}\left[f_{\ast}A,g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{\left[g_{\ast}f_{\ast}A,K\right]}

commutes for any
A\in\mathcal{W}_{X}
, where
\zeta=\mathrm{Tr}\phi
, cf. Theorem
3.2
(T1). This follows directly from the following the diagram

\textstyle{g_{\ast}f_{\ast}\left[A,f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{(gf)_{\ast}\left[A,f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{(gf)_{\ast}\left[A,(gf)^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{g_{\ast}\left[f_{\ast}A,f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\left[g_{\ast}f_{\ast}A,g_{\ast}f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 5$}}}
\textstyle{\left[(gf)_{\ast}A,(gf)_{\ast}(gf)^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{g_{\ast}\left[f_{\ast}A,g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\left[g_{\ast}f_{\ast}A,g_{\ast}g^{\Delta}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{\left[g_{\ast}f_{\ast}A,K\right]}

where
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:push_comp_dual}$}}
commutes by Theorem
3.2
(T2).
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper morphism and let
J
be a residue complex on
Y
, then the following diagram of spectra

\textstyle{G(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,f^{\Delta}J)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}
\textstyle{G(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y,J)}

commutes, where
G(X):=K(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)))
and
H
is the hyperbolic functor (cf.
[
Sch17
, Theorem 6.1]
).

Proof.

This follows from the following diagram

\textstyle{G(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{K(\mathcal{A}_{f})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{G(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,f^{\Delta}J)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(\mathcal{A}_{f},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}J})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y,J)}

where both squares commute by the functoriality of algebraic Bott sequences (cf.
[
Sch17
, Section 6]
).
∎

4.
Pullbacks for Hermitian
K
-theory

4.1.
On the functor
\mathrm{E}

Let
X
be a scheme admitting a dualizing complex
K
, and
\mu_{K}:X\to\mathbb{Z}
be the associated codimension function.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf on
X
. Suppose that
Z
is a closed subset of
X
, and
U
is an open subset of
X
. Recall the sections of
\mathcal{F}
on
U
with supports in
Z
are defined to be

\Gamma_{Z}(U,\mathcal{F}):=\Big\{s\in\Gamma(U)\mid\mathrm{Supp}(s)\subseteq Z\cap U\Big\}

where
\mathrm{Supp}(s):=\{x\in U\mid s_{x}\neq 0\}
. Define
\underline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F})
to be the sheaf

U\mapsto\Gamma_{Z}(U,\mathcal{F})
.
1
1

1

The presheaf
\underline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F})
is already a sheaf
[
Har67
]
.

Let
Z^{i}:=\{x\in X\mid\mu_{K}(x)\geq i\}
. Assume further that
K
is represented by a complex
I
of injective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Then, we have a filtration of closed subsets

\emptyset=Z^{m}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z^{p+1}\subseteq Z^{p}\subseteq Z^{p-1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z^{n}=X,

and a filtration of subcomplexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules

\cdots\subseteq\underline{\Gamma}_{Z_{p+1}}(I^{\bullet})\subseteq\underline{\Gamma}_{Z_{p}}(I^{\bullet})\subseteq\underline{\Gamma}_{Z_{p-1}}(I^{\bullet})\subseteq\cdots.

Define

\underline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{p+q}):=\underline{\Gamma}_{Z_{p}}(I^{p+q})/\underline{\Gamma}_{Z_{p+1}}(I^{p+q})

for any
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
. The differential on
I
induces a morphism of complexes

\cdots\rightarrow\underline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{p+q-1})\xrightarrow{\delta^{p,q-1}}\underline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{p+q})\xrightarrow{\delta^{p,q}}\underline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{p+q+1})\rightarrow\cdots

Definition 4.2
.

The local cohomology sheaf
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{\bullet})
is defined to be
\ker(\delta^{p,q})/\mathrm{im}(\delta^{p,q-1})
.

Definition 4.3

(
[
Har66
, p.225 Variation 8]
)
.

Let
x\in X
and
\mathcal{F}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Recall that
\Gamma_{x}(\mathcal{F})
consists of elements
\overline{s}
in the stalk
\mathcal{F}_{x}
, which have a representative
s\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{F}|_{U})
in a suitable neighborhood
U
of
x
, whose support is
\overline{\{x\}}\cap U
. Denote by
\mathrm{H}^{q}_{x}(I^{\bullet}):=\mathrm{H}^{q}(\Gamma_{x}(I^{\bullet}))
.

Theorem 4.4
.

There is a canonical functorial isomorphism

\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{\bullet})\cong\coprod_{\mu(x)=p}i_{x}(\mathrm{H}^{p+q}_{x}(I^{\bullet}))

Proof.

See
[
Har66
, p.225 Motif F]
.
∎

Notice that the differential on
\delta^{p,q}
induces a well-defined map
\ker(\delta^{p,q})\to\ker(\delta^{p+1,q})
, which induces a map on local cohomology

d:\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{\bullet})\to\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q+1}_{Z^{p+1}/Z^{p+2}}(I^{\bullet})

which yields a complex

\cdots\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q-1}_{Z^{p-1}/Z^{p}}(I^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{d}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(I^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{d}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{p+q+1}_{Z^{p+1}/Z^{p+2}}(I^{\bullet})\rightarrow\cdots

When
q=0
, we have the following definition.

Definition 4.5

(
[
Har66
, p.241]
)
.

The Cousin complex
E(K)
of dualizing complex
K
is defined to be the residue complex

\cdots\rightarrow\coprod_{\mu(x)=p-1}i_{x}(\mathrm{H}^{p-1}_{x}(I^{\bullet}))\xrightarrow{d}\coprod_{\mu(x)=p}i_{x}(\mathrm{H}^{p}_{x}(I^{\bullet}))\xrightarrow{d}\coprod_{\mu(x)=p+1}i_{x}(\mathrm{H}^{p+1}_{x}(I^{\bullet}))\rightarrow\cdots

Remark 4.6
.

Let
\mathrm{Dual}(X)_{Z^{\bullet}}
be the category of dualizing complexes in
D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
with respect to the filtration
Z^{\bullet}
(cf.
[
Har66
, p.284]
), and let
\mathrm{Res}(X)_{Z^{\bullet}}
be the additive category of residue complexes. Then, the functor

\mathrm{E}:\mathrm{Dual}(X)_{Z^{\bullet}}\to\mathrm{Res}(X)_{Z^{\bullet}}

that sends a dualizing complex to its Cousin complex is an equivalence of categories, cf.
[
Har66
, Proposition VI.1.1(c), p.304]
(and
[
Con00
, Lemma 3.2.1]
for clarification)). There is a canonical natural isomorphism
\mathrm{id}\to E
from
\mathrm{Dual}(X)_{Z^{\bullet}}
to itself, cf.
loc. cit.
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat morphism. Let
J,\tilde{J}\in\mathrm{Res}(Y)_{Z^{\bullet}}
. Assume that
f^{\ast}J
is a dualizing complex on
X
. Then,
f^{*}\tilde{J}
is a dualizing complex with the same associated codimension function as
f^{\ast}J
. Moreover, the canonical morphism

\textnormal{Hom}_{Z^{0}\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}(f^{\ast}\tilde{J},Ef^{\ast}J)\to\textnormal{Hom}_{D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}(f^{\ast}\tilde{J},Ef^{\ast}J).

is a bijection.

Proof.

Since
f^{\ast}J
is a dualizing complex,
Ef^{\ast}J
is degreewise injective and bounded. Thus, the canonical morphism

\textnormal{Hom}_{K^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}(f^{\ast}\tilde{J},Ef^{\ast}J)\to\textnormal{Hom}_{D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}(f^{\ast}\tilde{J},Ef^{\ast}J).

is an isomorphism, cf.
[
Wei94
, Corollary 10.4.7]
. All we need to do is to prove that

\textnormal{Hom}_{X}((f^{\ast}\tilde{J})^{p},(Ef^{\ast}J)^{p-1})=0

for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The question is local on
X
. Assume that
X=\mathrm{Spec}(S)
and
Y=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
. By assumption,
J^{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
is a dualizing complex on
X
. Let
E(J^{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S)
be the associated Cousin complex of the
S
-module
J^{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
. Denote the codimension function of the dualizing complex
J^{\bullet}
(resp.
J^{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S
) by
\mu:\mathrm{Spec}(S)\to\mathbb{Z}
(resp.
\mu^{\prime}:\mathrm{Spec}(R)\to\mathbb{Z}
).

Take any morphism
t\in\textnormal{Hom}_{S}(\tilde{J}^{p}\otimes_{R}S,E^{p-1}(J^{\bullet}\otimes_{R}S))
. Suppose that
t\neq 0
, then there exists
r\in\tilde{J}^{p}
with
\mathrm{Supp}(rR)=V(\mathfrak{p})
for some prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
in
R
satisfying
\mu(\mathfrak{p})=p
, and
t(r\otimes 1)\neq 0
. It follows that the support of
t(r\otimes 1)
is a finite union
V(\mathfrak{q}_{1})\cup\ldots\cup V(\mathfrak{q}_{n})
with

(6)

\mu^{\prime}(\mathfrak{q}_{i})=p-1

and
\mathfrak{q}_{i}\in\mathrm{Spec}(S)
. Since
f
is flat, the support of
(r\otimes 1)S\subseteq J^{p}\otimes_{R}S
is
\mathrm{Supp}(rR\otimes_{R}S)
, which is
f^{-1}\mathrm{Supp}(rR)=V(\mathfrak{p}S)
, cf.
[
Bou72
, Proposition 19, p.107]
.
Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a minimal prime ideal in
S
containing
\mathfrak{p}S
. Since
f
is flat,
\mathfrak{p}=f^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})
by the going-down property (cf.
[
Eis95
, Lemma 10.11, p.239]
). Note that
S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}}
is an artinian local ring. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal of
S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Note that
\mathfrak{m}
is an
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module of finite length. As
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
-modules,
\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathfrak{q}S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}}\cong\kappa(\mathfrak{p})\otimes_{R_{\mathfrak{p}}}S_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Set
\omega:=(J^{\bullet})_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{R_{\mathfrak{p}}}S_{\mathfrak{q}}
. It is a dualizing complex, since the localization of a dualizing complex is dualizing, cf.
[
Har66
, Lemma V.2.3, p.259]
. The short exact sequence of
S_{\mathfrak{q}}
-modules

0\to\mathfrak{m}\to S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}}\to\kappa(\mathfrak{q})\to 0

induces a long exact sequence

\displaystyle\cdots\to\mathrm{Ext}^{q-1}_{S_{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}},\omega)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{q-1}_{S_{\mathfrak{q}}}(\mathfrak{m},\omega)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{q}_{S_{\mathfrak{q}}}(\kappa(\mathfrak{q}),\omega)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{q}_{S_{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}},\omega)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ext}^{q}_{S_{\mathfrak{q}}}(\mathfrak{m},\omega)\to\cdots

Note that

\mathrm{Ext}^{q}_{S_{\mathfrak{q}}}(S_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}S_{\mathfrak{q}},\omega)\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{q}_{R_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\kappa(\mathfrak{p}),(J^{\bullet})_{\mathfrak{p}})\otimes_{R_{\mathfrak{p}}}S_{\mathfrak{q}}=0,\quad\text{ for all }q\neq p.

Hence
\mathrm{Ext}^{q}(\kappa(\mathfrak{p}),\omega)\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{q-1}(\mathfrak{m},\omega)
for all
q<p
and
\mathrm{Ext}^{q+1}(\kappa(\mathfrak{p}),\omega)\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{q}(\mathfrak{m},\omega)
for all
q>p
.
Since
\omega
is a dualizing complex,
\mathrm{Ext}^{q}(\kappa(\mathfrak{p}),\omega)=0
and
\mathrm{Ext}^{q}(\mathfrak{m},\omega)=\textnormal{Hom}^{q}(\mathfrak{m},E(\kappa(\mathfrak{q}))[\mu^{\prime}(\mathfrak{q})])=0
if
q\neq\mu^{\prime}(\mathfrak{q})
(cf.
[
Sta23
,
Lemma 0A7Q
]
).
It follows that
\mathrm{Ext}^{q}(\kappa(\mathfrak{p}),\omega)=0
for all
q\neq p
. Thus
\mu^{\prime}(\mathfrak{q})=p
.

Therefore for any
\mathfrak{q}
in
V(\mathfrak{p}S)
,
\mu^{\prime}(\mathfrak{q})\geq p
(cf.
[
Har66
, proposition V.7.1]
) and the equality holds if and only if
\mathfrak{q}
is a minimal prime ideal containing
\mathfrak{p}S
. Since
\mathrm{Supp}(t(r\otimes 1))\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}(r\otimes 1)\subseteq V(\mathfrak{p}S)
, this contradicts Equation (
6
).
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat morphism. Let
J
be a residue complex on
Y
. Assume
f^{\ast}J
is a dualizing complex on
X
. Denote by
\mu:Y\to\mathbb{Z}
(resp.
\mu^{\prime}:X\to\mathbb{Z}
) the codimension function of dualizing complexes
J
(resp.
f^{\ast}J
). Then
\mu^{\prime}(x)\geq\mu(f(x))
for any
x\in X
and the equality holds if and only
x
is a generic point of
\overline{\{f^{-1}(f(x))\}}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Lemma
4.7
implies that any natural isomorphism
f^{*}(-)\to Ef^{*}(-)
(which exists, cf.
[
Har66
, Proof of Proposition IV.3.4, p.249]
) from
\mathrm{Res}(Y)_{Z^{\bullet}}
to
D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
has a unique lifting which is a natural quasi-isomorphism from
\mathrm{Res}(Y)_{Z^{\bullet}}
to
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
. We fix a natural quasi-isomorphism

\rho:f^{*}(-)\to Ef^{*}(-)

from
\mathrm{Res}(Y)_{Z^{\bullet}}
to
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
.

4.10.
Pullback of flat morphisms

Let
f:X\to Y
be a flat morphism throughout this subsection. Assume that
J
is a residue complex on
Y
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Suppose that
f^{\ast}J
is a dualizing complex on
X
. Then the exact dg functor

f^{\ast}:\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))\to\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))

induces a map of Grothendieck-Witt spectra

f^{\ast}:\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,J\right)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,Ef^{\ast}J\right)

which respects composition, i.e. if
f
and
g
are two consecutive flat morphisms, then
f^{\ast}g^{\ast}=(gf)^{\ast}
.

Remark 4.12
.

Note that
f^{*}J
is a residue complex, if
f
is residually stable, i.e.
f
is Gorenstein, the fibres of
f
are discrete and for all
x\in X
, the extension
\kappa(x)/\kappa(f(x))
is algebraic (e.g. open immersion), cf.
[
Har66
, Proposition VI.5.3, p.352]
(and
[
Con00
, p.132]
for clarification). If
f
is a surjective Gorenstein morphism of finite type, then
f^{*}J
is a dualizing complex if and only if
J
is a dualizing complex, cf.
[
Sta23
,
Lemma 0E4N
]
.

Lemma 4.13
.

The canonical natural transformation

\beta:f^{\ast}\left[-,J\right]_{Y}\to\left[f^{\ast}-,f^{\ast}J\right]_{X}.

from
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))
to
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
induces a natural quasi-isomorphism

\tilde{\beta}:f^{\ast}\left[-,J\right]_{Y}\xrightarrow{\beta}\left[f^{\ast}-,f^{\ast}J\right]_{X}\xrightarrow{\rho}\left[f^{*}-,Ef^{\ast}J\right]_{X}.

Proof.

Since the complex
Ef^{*}J
is degreewise injective and represents
f^{*}J
, we have

\mathrm{R}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(-,f^{*}J)\cong\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(-,Ef^{*}J)

in
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
. The result follows from
[
Har66
, Proposition II.5.8]
.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of schemes, and let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules. Then, the following diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}\textnormal{can}}
can
\textstyle{f^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{F},f^{\ast}\mathcal{G}),f^{\ast}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),f^{\ast}\mathcal{G})}

commutes in
\mathbf{M}(X)
.

Proof.

By the adjunction of
f^{\ast}
and
f_{\ast}
, it suffices to prove that the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
can
\scriptstyle{\textnormal{can}\eta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta\eta}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{F},f^{\ast}\mathcal{G}),f^{\ast}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}\beta^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{f_{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),f^{\ast}\mathcal{G})}

commutes in
\mathbf{M}(Y)
, where the natural transformation
\eta:\mathrm{id}\to f_{\ast}f^{\ast}
is the unit of the adjunction. We check the commutativity locally by sections. Let
V
be an open subset of
Y
. We show that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
can
\scriptstyle{\textnormal{can}\eta}
\textstyle{\textnormal{Hom}_{V}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})|_{V},\mathcal{G}|_{V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta\eta}
\textstyle{\textnormal{Hom}_{f^{-1}V}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{F},f^{\ast}\mathcal{G})|_{f^{-1}V},f^{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{f^{-1}V})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\textnormal{Hom}_{f^{-1}V}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})|_{f^{-1}V},f^{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{f^{-1}V})}

commutes. Let
s\in\mathcal{F}(V)
. We explain the identity
\beta\eta(\textnormal{can}(s))=\beta^{\sharp}(\textnormal{can}\eta(s))
. Take an open subset
U
of
f^{-1}V
. The left-hand side morphism

\beta\eta(\textnormal{can}(s)):f^{*}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(U)\to f^{*}\mathcal{G}(U)
sends

t\otimes u\in f^{*}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(U):=f^{-1}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\otimes_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)

to the element
t(s)\otimes u\in f^{*}\mathcal{G}(U)\cong f^{-1}\mathcal{G}\otimes_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
. Strictly speaking, one should express
f^{-1}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
as a colimit and take a representative of
t
to define the element
t(s)
. For the right-hand side, note first that the canonical morphism
\beta:f^{*}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(U)\to\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{*}\mathcal{F},f^{*}\mathcal{G})(U)
sends an element
t\otimes u
to the morphism
\beta(t\otimes u):f^{\ast}\mathcal{F}|_{U}\to f^{\ast}\mathcal{G}|_{U}
such that
\beta(t\otimes u)(r\otimes v):=t(r)\otimes uv
. Hence,
\beta^{\sharp}(\textnormal{can}\eta(s))(t\otimes u)=\textnormal{can}(s\otimes 1)(\beta(t\otimes u))=\beta(t\otimes u)(s\otimes 1)=t(s)\otimes u
.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

The pullback functor

f^{\ast}:\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))\to\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))

induces a dg form functor

(f^{\ast},\tilde{\beta}):(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\sharp_{J})\to(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\sharp_{Ef^{\ast}J}).

Proof.

All we need to do is demonstrate that the diagram of functors

\textstyle{f^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}_{f^{\ast}}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}\sharp_{J}\sharp_{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}_{\sharp_{J}}}
\textstyle{\sharp_{Ef^{\ast}J}\sharp_{Ef^{\ast}J}f^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp_{Ef^{\ast}J}(\tilde{\beta})}
\textstyle{\sharp_{Ef^{\ast}J}f^{\ast}\sharp_{J}}

commutes. The proof requires that we establish the commutativity of the following diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 7$}}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}[[A,J],J]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 8$}}}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f^{\ast}A,f^{\ast}J\right],f^{\ast}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 9$}}}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}[A,J],f^{\ast}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f^{\ast}A,Ef^{\ast}J\right],Ef^{\ast}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\left[\left[f^{\ast}A,f^{\ast}J\right],Ef^{\ast}J\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}[A,J],Ef^{\ast}J\right]}

for any object
A
in
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y))
. The diagram
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:pull_dual_1}$}}
(resp.
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:pull_dual_2}$}}
) is commutative by Lemma
4.14
(resp. Lemma
3.7
). The diagram
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:pull_dual_3}$}}
commutes by naturality.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.11
.

By Lemma
4.13
and Proposition
4.15
, we have a nonsingular exact form functor
(f^{\ast},\tilde{\beta}):(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\textnormal{quis},\sharp_{J})\to(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\textnormal{quis},\sharp_{Ef^{\ast}J})
, which induces the required map

f^{\ast}:\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,J\right)\to\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,Ef^{\ast}J\right).

The fact that the pullback morphism respects composition follows from Proposition
4.17
below.
∎

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be flat morphisms. Let
K
be a residue complex on
Z
. Assume that
g^{\ast}K
and
f^{\ast}g^{\ast}K
are both dualizing complexes. Let
a:f^{\ast}g^{\ast}\to(gf)^{\ast}
be the canonical natural isomorphism, cf.
[
Lip09
,
(3.6.1)^{*}
, p.118]
.
The functor
E:\mathrm{Dual}(X)\to\mathrm{Res}(X)
sends the isomorphism
f^{*}(\rho)
to an isomorphism
E(f^{*}(\rho)):Ef^{\ast}g^{\ast}K\to Ef^{\ast}Eg^{\ast}K
. Define
b
to be the composition

b:E(gf)^{*}K\xrightarrow{E(a^{-1})}Ef^{*}g^{*}K\xrightarrow{E(f^{*}(\rho))}Ef^{*}Eg^{*}K,

which is an isomorphism.

Lemma 4.16
.

The diagram

\textstyle{f^{*}g^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}(\rho)}
\textstyle{(gf)^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{E(gf)^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{f^{*}Eg^{*}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{Ef^{*}Eg^{*}K}

commutes in
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.7
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}((fg)^{*}K,E(fg)^{*}K)\cong\textnormal{Hom}_{D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}((fg)^{*}K,E(fg)^{*}K).

Since
f^{*}g^{*}K\cong(gf)^{*}K
and
Ef^{*}Eg^{*}K\cong E(gf)^{*}K
in
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
, we see that the canonical morphism

\textnormal{Hom}_{\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}(f^{*}g^{*}K,Ef^{*}Eg^{*}K)\to\textnormal{Hom}_{D^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))}(f^{*}g^{*}K,Ef^{*}Eg^{*}K)

is a bijection. The diagram in question is commutative (i.e.
b\rho a=\rho f^{*}(\rho)
) in
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
by naturality, and therefore it is commutative in
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))
.
∎

Proposition 4.17

(Composition of flat pullback)
.

The following diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Z,K\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(gf)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,E(gf)^{\ast}K\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(Y,Eg^{\ast}K\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X,Ef^{\ast}Eg^{\ast}K\right)}

commutes.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that the following diagram of form functor

\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Z)),\sharp_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g^{\ast},\tilde{\beta})}
\scriptstyle{((gf)^{\ast},\tilde{\beta})}
\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\sharp_{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id},b)}
\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\sharp_{Eg^{\ast}K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f^{\ast},\tilde{\beta})}
\textstyle{(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X)),\sharp_{I})}

commutes up to a natural weak equivalence of form functors. It suffices to prove that the following diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}g^{\ast}\left[A,K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 10$}}}
\textstyle{(gf)^{\ast}\left[A,K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\left[(gf)^{\ast}A,(gf)^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\left[(gf)^{\ast}A,E(gf)^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}\left[g^{\ast}A,g^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}g^{\ast}A,f^{\ast}g^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 11$}}}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}g^{\ast}A,(gf)^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}g^{\ast}A,E(gf)^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}\left[g^{\ast}A,Eg^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}g^{\ast}A,f^{\ast}Eg^{\ast}K\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left[f^{\ast}g^{\ast}A,Ef^{\ast}Eg^{\ast}K\right]}

commutes for any
A\in\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Z))
, where
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:pull_comp_1}$}}
commutes by Lemma
4.18
and
{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle\ref{diag:pull_comp_2}$}}
commutes by Lemma
4.16
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

For any
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
-modules
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
, the following diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}g^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Z}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{(gf)^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Z}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}((gf)^{\ast}\mathcal{F},(gf)^{\ast}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{Y}(g^{\ast}\mathcal{F},g^{\ast}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}g^{\ast}\mathcal{F},f^{\ast}g^{\ast}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{om}_{X}(f^{\ast}g^{\ast}\mathcal{F},(gf)^{\ast}\mathcal{G})}

commutes.

Proof.

See
[
Lip09
, p.126 Exercise 3.7.1.1]
.
∎

5.
On the base change formula

In this section, we prove a base change formula. Let

\textstyle{{X^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{g}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

be a fibre product. Then, we have a canonical natural transformation

\varepsilon:g^{*}f_{*}\to\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}

from
\mathbf{M}(X)
to
\mathbf{M}(Y^{\prime})
, which is adjoint to the morphism
f_{\ast}\to f_{\ast}\bar{g}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}=g_{\ast}\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}
, cf.
[
GW20
, Section 12.2, p.327]
. If
f
is proper and
g
is flat, then the canonical morphism
\varepsilon:g^{*}f_{*}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism for any quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
, cf.
loc. cit.
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Assume that
f
is proper and
g
is an open immersion. Then there is a natural isomorphism

\gamma:\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}\xrightarrow{\cong}\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}

from
\mathrm{Res}(Y)
to
\mathrm{Res}(X^{\prime})
such that the following diagram

\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{g^{\ast}f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{g^{\ast}}

commutes.

Proof.

Note that residue complexes are degreewise quasi-coherent, so
\varepsilon
is an isomorphism. See
[
Har66
, Theorem VI.5.3, p.352, 5.5, p.354 and 5.6, p.355]
and
[
Con00
, Lemma 3.4.3, p.149]
.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Assume that
f
is proper and
g
is an open immersion. Then, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X^{\prime},\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}I\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}\left(X^{\prime},\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}I\right)}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(X,f^{\Delta}I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{g}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y^{\prime},g^{\ast}I)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{GW}^{[n]}(Y,I)}
\scriptstyle{g^{\ast}}

of Grothendieck-Witt spectra commutes up to homotopy.

Proof.

We first show that the functor
\bar{g}^{\ast}:\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X))\to\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(X^{\prime}))
satisfies
\bar{g}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{f})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\bar{f}}
. By
[
Lip09
, Proposition 3.9.5, p.142]
and
[
SGA6
, Proposition IV 3.1.0, p.290]
, we note that
\varepsilon:g^{*}\mathrm{R}f_{*}\to\mathrm{R}\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}
is a natural isomorphism from
\mathrm{D}_{c}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
to
\mathrm{D}_{c}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(Y^{\prime}))
. Consider the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{g^{*}f_{*}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{g^{*}\mathrm{R}f_{*}(A)}

in
\mathrm{D}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y^{\prime}))
where
A
is an object in
\mathrm{Ch}_{c}^{b}(\mathbf{M}(X))
, cf.
[
Lip09
, Lemma 3.10.1.1, p.146]
. From this diagram, it is evident that the canonical morphism
\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}(A)\to\mathrm{R}\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}(A)
is an isomorphism if the canonical morphism
A\to\mathrm{R}f_{*}A
is an isomorphism. Therefore,
\bar{g}^{*}(A)
is in
\mathcal{A}_{\bar{f}}
, if
A
is in the category
\mathcal{A}_{f}
. Moreover, it becomes clear that
\varepsilon:g^{*}f_{*}\to\bar{f}_{*}\bar{g}^{*}
is a natural quasi-isomorphism from
\mathcal{A}_{f}
to
\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y^{\prime}))
.

All we need to do is demonstrate that the diagram

\textstyle{\left(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{f}},\sharp_{\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}I}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\bar{f}_{\ast},\zeta)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{f}},\sharp_{\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}I}\right)}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id},\gamma)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\left(\mathcal{A}_{f},\sharp_{f^{\Delta}I}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\bar{g}^{\ast},\beta)}
\scriptstyle{(f_{\ast},\zeta)}
\textstyle{\left(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y^{\prime})),\sharp_{g^{\ast}I}\right)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\left(\textnormal{Ch}^{b}_{c}(\mathbf{M}(Y)),\sharp_{I}\right)}
\scriptstyle{(g^{\ast},\beta)}

commutes up to the natural quasi-isomorphism
\varepsilon
. The argument is finished, if we can prove the diagram

\textstyle{g^{\ast}f_{\ast}[A,f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{g^{\ast}[f_{\ast}A,I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{[g^{\ast}f_{\ast}A,g^{\ast}I]}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}[A,f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}[\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}[\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{[\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{g}^{\ast}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\sharp}}

commutes for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{f}
. This follows from the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}[A,f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 12$}}}
\textstyle{g^{\ast}f_{\ast}[A,f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}[\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{[\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm\framebox{$\scriptstyle 13$}}}
\textstyle{[\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}A,g^{\ast}f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{[g^{\ast}f_{\ast}A,g^{\ast}f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{g^{\ast}[f_{\ast}A,f_{\ast}f^{\Delta}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{\ast}[\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{[\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}A,\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{f}^{\Delta}g^{\ast}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Tr}}
\textstyle{[\bar{f}_{\ast}\bar{g}^{\ast}A,g^{\ast}I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{[g^{\ast}f_{\ast}A,g^{\ast}I]}
\textstyle{g^{\ast}[f_{\ast}A,I]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\beta}

where
